### Language

Main article: [Languages of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Israel)



[Road sign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Road_signs_in_Israel) in [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language), [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language_in_Israel), and [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language)

The official language is [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language). Hebrew is the primary language of the state and is spoken daily by the majority of the population. Prior to 1948, [opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Yiddish_sentiment) to [Yiddish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiddish), the historical language of the Ashkenazi Jews, was common among supporters of the Zionist movement, including the Yishuv, who sought to promote [Hebrew's revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revival_of_the_Hebrew_language) as a unifying national language.[^cite_note-650] These sentiments were reflected in the early policies of the Israeli government, which largely banned [Yiddish theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiddish_theatre) and publications.[^cite_note-651] Until 2018, [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language_in_Israel) was also an official language;[^cite_note-lang1-15] in 2018 it was downgraded to having a "special status in the state".[^cite_note-lang2-13][^cite_note-lang3-14] Arabic is spoken by the Arab minority and is the main medium of teaching in Arab schools where Hebrew is studied as a subject.[^cite_note-arabic_lang-652] Arabic is studied in most Jewish schools and is often used on signage and in transport announcements.[^cite_note-653]&#x20;

Due to mass immigration from the former Soviet Union and [Ethiopia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aliyah_from_Ethiopia) (some 130,000 [Ethiopian Jews live in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_Jews_in_Israel)),[^cite_note-The_Ethiopian_Population_In_Israel-654][^cite_note-655] [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language_in_Israel) and [Amharic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amharic) are widely spoken.[^cite_note-656] Over one million Russian-speaking immigrants arrived in Israel between 1990 and 2004.[^cite_note-657] French is spoken by around 700,000 Israelis,[^cite_note-658] mostly originating [from France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Jews_in_Israel) and North Africa (see [Maghrebi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghrebi_Jews)). English was an official language during the Mandate period;[^cite_note-659] it lost this status after the establishment of Israel, but retains a role comparable to that of an official language.[^cite_note-660][^cite_note-661] Many Israelis communicate reasonably well in English, as many television programmes are broadcast in English with subtitles and the language is taught in elementary school. The primary medium of teaching in Israeli universities is Hebrew, but some courses are offered in English.[^cite_note-662]&#x20;

The main language of the [Circassians in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians_in_Israel) is [Adyghe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adyghe_language), and most of them also know Hebrew and Arabic.&#x20;

The deaf community in Israel primarily uses [Israeli Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Sign_Language). Several [village sign languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Village_sign_language) are used in Israel, most notably the [Al-Sayyid Bedouin Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Sayyid_Bedouin_Sign_Language) and the [Kafr Qasem Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafr_Qasem_Sign_Language).&#x20;

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Israel)

See also: [Abrahamic religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrahamic_religions)

A large open area with people bounded by old stone walls. To the left is a mosque with large golden dome.

The [Dome of the Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome_of_the_Rock) and the [Western Wall](75225.md), Jerusalem

The estimated religious affiliation as of 2022 was 73.5% Jewish, 18.1% [Muslim](19541.md), 1.9% [Christian](18337522.md), 1.6% [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze), and 4.9% other.[^cite_note-cia-19] The [religious affiliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_religious_movements) of [Israeli Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Jews) varies widely: a 2016 survey by [Pew Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research) indicates that 49% self-identify as [Hiloni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiloni) (secular), 29% as [Masorti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masortim) (traditional), 13% as [Dati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dati) (religious) and 9% as [Haredi](53835.md) (ultra-Orthodox).[^cite_note-663] Haredi Jews are expected to represent over 20% of the Jewish population by 2028.[^cite_note-664] [Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Israel) constitute the largest religious minority, making up about 18.1% of the population. About 1.9% of the population is [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Israel), and 1.6% is [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze_in_Israel).[^cite_note-CIA2022-665] The Christian population comprises primarily [Arab Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Christians) and [Aramean Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arameans_in_Israel) but also includes post-Soviet immigrants, foreign labourers, [Armenian Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenians_in_Israel_and_Palestine), and followers of [Messianic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messianic_Judaism), considered by most Christians and Jews to be a form of Christianity.[^cite_note-666] Members of many other religious groups, including [Buddhists](3267529.md) and [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Israel), maintain a presence in Israel, albeit in small numbers.[^cite_note-667] Out of over one million immigrants from the former Soviet Union, about 300,000 are considered not Jewish by the [Chief Rabbinate of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Rabbinate_of_Israel).[^cite_note-668]&#x20;

Israel comprises a major part of the [Holy Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Land), a region of significant importance to all [Abrahamic religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrahamic_religions). Jerusalem is of [special importance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_significance_of_Jerusalem) to Jews, Muslims, and Christians, as it is the home of [sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_places_in_Jerusalem) that are pivotal to their religious beliefs, such as the [Old City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_City_\(Jerusalem\)) that incorporates the [Western Wall](75225.md) and the [Temple Mount](65719.md) (Al-Aqsa Mosque compound) and the [Church of the Holy Sepulchre](7810.md).[^cite_note-669] Other locations of religious importance are [Nazareth](21702.md) (site of the [Annunciation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annunciation) of [Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_\(mother_of_Jesus\))), [Tiberias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiberias) and [Safed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safed) (two of the [Four Holy Cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Holy_Cities) in Judaism), the [White Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Mosque,_Ramla) in [Ramla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramla) (shrine of the prophet [Saleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salih)), and the [Church of Saint George and Mosque of Al-Khadr, Lod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Saint_George_and_Mosque_of_Al-Khadr,_Lod) (tomb of [Saint George](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_George) or [Al Khidr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Khidr)). A number of other religious landmarks are located in the [West Bank](33209.md), including [Joseph's Tomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph%27s_Tomb), the [birthplace of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_Nativity), [Rachel's Tomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel%27s_Tomb), and the [Cave of the Patriarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cave_of_the_Patriarchs). The [administrative center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arc_\(Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD\)) of the [Baháʼí Faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith) and the [Shrine of the Báb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrine_of_the_B%C3%A1b) are located at the [Baháʼí World Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_World_Centre) in [Haifa](55606.md); the leader of the faith is [buried](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrine_of_Bah%C3%A1%27u%27ll%C3%A1h) in [Acre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acre,_Israel).[^cite_note-670][^cite_note-671][^cite_note-672] The [Mahmood Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmood_Mosque,_Haifa) is affiliated with the reformist [Ahmadiyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadiyya_in_Israel) movement. [Kababir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kababir), Haifa's mixed neighbourhood of Jews and Ahmadi Arabs, is one of a few of its kind in the country.[^cite_note-673][^cite_note-674]&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Israel)



[Multidisciplinary Brain Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar-Ilan_University#Leslie_and_Susan_Gonda_Multidisciplinary_Brain_Research_Center) at [Bar-Ilan University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar-Ilan_University)

In 2015, Israel [ranked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_tertiary_education_attainment) third among OECD members for the percentage of 25–64-year-olds that have attained tertiary education with 49% compared with the OECD average of 35%.[^cite_note-OECD_education-675] In 2012, the country ranked third in the number of academic degrees per capita (20 per cent of the population).[^cite_note-676]&#x20;

Israel has a [school life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_life_expectancy) of 16 years and a [literacy rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_literacy_rate) of 97.8%.[^cite_note-cia-19] The State Education Law (1953) established five types of schools: state secular, state religious, ultra orthodox, communal settlement schools, and Arab schools. The public secular is the largest school group and is attended by the majority of Jewish and non-Arab pupils. Most Arabs send their children to Arabic-language schools.[^cite_note-arabic_lang-652] Education is compulsory between the ages of three and eighteen.[^cite_note-677] Schooling is divided into three tiers—primary school (grades 1–6), middle school (grades 7–9), and high school (grades 10–12)—culminating with *[Bagrut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagrut)* matriculation exams. Proficiency in core subjects such as mathematics, the Hebrew language, Hebrew and general literature, the English language, history, Biblical scripture and civics is necessary to receive a Bagrut certificate.[^cite_note-moia-678]&#x20;

The Jewish population maintains a relatively high level of educational attainment where just under half of all Israeli Jews (46%) hold post-secondary degrees.[^cite_note-679][^cite_note-680] Israeli Jews 25 and older have an average 11.6 years of schooling, making them one of the most highly educated of all major religious groups in the world.[^cite_note-681][^cite_note-682] In Arab, Christian and Druze schools, the exam on Biblical studies is replaced by an exam on Muslim, Christian or Druze heritage, respectively.[^cite_note-683] In 2020, 68.7% of 12th graders earned a matriculation certificate.[^cite_note-684]&#x20;



[Mount Scopus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Scopus) Campus of the [Hebrew University of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_University_of_Jerusalem)

Israel has a tradition of higher education where its university education has been largely responsible in spurring modern economic development.[^cite_note-685] Israel has [nine public universities subsidised by the state and 49 private colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Israeli_universities_and_colleges).[^cite_note-moia-678][^cite_note-686][^cite_note-687] The [Hebrew University of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_University_of_Jerusalem) houses the [National Library of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Israel), the world's largest repository of Judaica and Hebraica.[^cite_note-688] The [Technion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technion) and the Hebrew University consistently ranked among world's 100 top universities by [ARWU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_Ranking_of_World_Universities) ranking.[^cite_note-ARWU-573] Other major universities include the [Weizmann Institute of Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weizmann_Institute_of_Science), [Tel Aviv University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_Aviv_University), [Ben-Gurion University of the Negev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben-Gurion_University_of_the_Negev), [Bar-Ilan University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bar-Ilan_University), the [University of Haifa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Haifa), and the [Open University of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_University_of_Israel).&#x20;

### Health

Main article: [Health in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Israel)



[Rabin Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabin_Medical_Center) in [Petah Tikva](202411.md), Israel

[Health in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Israel) is advanced and efficient compared to other developed countries; Israel spends 7.3% of their gross domestic product on health compared to 9.3% in the OECD and 18% in the US. With the passing of the 1995 National Healthcare Law, there is mandated universal healthcare for 100% of the population funded by a health tax which is a fixed percentage of income.[^cite_note-wi-689][^cite_note-690] This health care 'basket' includes a list of services mandated by the Ministry of health. Individuals can purchase coverage for additional services for an extra charge.[^cite_note-691] Despite the cost savings in healthcare, the health status in Israel is satisfactory compared to other countries.[^cite_note-wi-689][^cite_note-692]&#x20;

The life expectancy in Israel is 83.8 years, above the [OECD](33853117.md) average by 2.7 years. Preventable mortality is 78 per 100,000, which is less than the OECD average of 145, whereas treatable mortality is 56 per 100,000, also less than the OECD average of 77. The suicide rate is 4 per 100,000, which is lower than the OECD average of 11. However, 9.1% of Israelis consider their health status bad or very bad, which is higher than the OECD average of 8.0%.[^cite_note-z261-693] Major risk factors include, smoking by 17.0 percent of the population, above the OECD average of 14.8 percent. Alcohol use is less at 2.7 liters per person compared to 8.5 liters. Twenty-eight percent of adults do not get exercise, a little below the 30 percent average. Obesity in Israel is 18 percent, near the OECDs 19 percent.[^cite_note-z261-693]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Israel)

Cultural diversity stems from its diverse population: Jews from various diaspora communities brought their cultural and religious traditions with them.[^cite_note-694] [Arab influences](492331.md#Culture) are found in [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Israel),[^cite_note-695] [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Israel),[^cite_note-696] and [cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_cuisine),[^cite_note-697] among others.[^cite_note-MendelRanta2016p137-698] Israel is the only country where life revolves around the [Hebrew calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_calendar), which is aligned to the seasons in the Levant. [Public holidays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Israel) are determined by the [Jewish holidays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_holidays), and except for [Yom HaAtzma'ut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_HaAtzma%27ut) (Independence Day) there are no annual civil holidays. The official day of rest is Saturday, the [Jewish Sabbath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabbat).[^cite_note-699]&#x20;

### Literature

[Israeli literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_literature) is primarily [poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Hebrew_poetry) and prose written in Hebrew, as part of the [renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revival_of_the_Hebrew_language) of Hebrew as a spoken language since the mid-19th century, although a small body of literature is published in other languages. By law, two copies of all works published in Israel must be deposited in the [National Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Israel).[^cite_note-700] In 2016, 89 per cent of the 7,300 books transferred to the library were in Hebrew.[^cite_note-701]&#x20;

In 1966, [Shmuel Yosef Agnon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shmuel_Yosef_Agnon) shared the [Nobel Prize in Literature](23385442.md) with German Jewish author [Nelly Sachs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelly_Sachs).[^cite_note-702] Leading poets include [Yehuda Amichai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yehuda_Amichai), [Nathan Alterman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathan_Alterman), [Leah Goldberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leah_Goldberg), and [Rachel Bluwstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel_Bluwstein).[^cite_note-703] Internationally famous contemporary novelists include [Amos Oz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amos_Oz), [Etgar Keret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etgar_Keret) and [David Grossman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Grossman).[^cite_note-704][^cite_note-705]&#x20;

### Music and dance

Further information: [Music of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Israel) and [Dance in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance_in_Israel)

Several dozen musicians in formal dress, holding their instruments, behind a conductor

[Israel Philharmonic Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Philharmonic_Orchestra) conducted by [Zubin Mehta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zubin_Mehta)

[Israeli music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Israel) includes [Mizrahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrahi_music) and [Sephardic music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_music), [Hasidic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasidic_Judaism) melodies, [Greek music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_music_in_Israel), [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz), and [pop rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pop_rock).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBroughtonEllinghamTrillo1999365–369-706][^cite_note-707] The [Israel Philharmonic Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Philharmonic_Orchestra)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBen-Sasson19851095-708][^cite_note-709] has been in operation for over seventy years and performs more than two hundred concerts each year.[^cite_note-710] [Itzhak Perlman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itzhak_Perlman), [Pinchas Zukerman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinchas_Zukerman) and [Ofra Haza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ofra_Haza) are among the internationally acclaimed musicians born in Israel. The country [has participated](3190362.md) in the [Eurovision Song Contest](9954.md) nearly every year since 1973, winning it four times and hosting three times.[^cite_note-history-711] [Eilat](66162.md) has hosted its own international music festival, the [Red Sea Jazz Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Sea_Jazz_Festival), every summer since 1987.[^cite_note-712] The nation's canonical [folk songs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_music) are known as "Songs of the Land of Israel".[^cite_note-713]&#x20;

### Cinema and theatre

Main article: [Cinema of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Israel)

Ten Israeli films [have been final nominees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Israeli_submissions_for_the_Academy_Award_for_Best_Foreign_Language_Film) for [Best Foreign Language Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Foreign_Language_Film) at the [Academy Awards](324.md). Palestinian Israeli filmmakers have made films dealing with the Arab-Israeli conflict and status of Palestinians within Israel, such as [Mohammad Bakri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Bakri)'s 2002 film *[Jenin, Jenin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jenin,_Jenin)* and *[The Syrian Bride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Syrian_Bride)*.&#x20;

Continuing the strong theatrical traditions of the [Yiddish theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yiddish_theatre) in Eastern Europe, Israel maintains a vibrant theatre scene. Founded in 1918, [Habima Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habima_Theatre) in Tel Aviv is Israel's oldest [repertory theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repertory_theatre) company and national theatre.[^cite_note-714] Other theatres include [Ohel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohel_Theater), [the Cameri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameri_Theatre) and [Gesher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gesher_Theater).[^cite_note-715][^cite_note-716]&#x20;

### Visual arts

Main article: [Visual arts in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visual_arts_in_Israel)



*[The Roaring Lion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Roaring_Lion_\(sculpture\))* sculpture by [Avraham Melnikov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avraham_Melnikov)

Israeli Jewish art has been particularly influenced by the [Kabbalah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabbalah), the [Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talmud) and the [Zohar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zohar). Another art movement that held a prominent role in the 20th century was the [School of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_of_Paris). In the late 19th and early 20th century, the Yishuv's art was dominated by art trends emanating [Bezalel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bezalel_Academy_of_Arts_and_Design). Beginning in the 1920s, the local art scene was heavily influenced by modern French art, first introduced by [Isaac Frenkel Frenel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitzhak_Frenkel).[^cite_note-717][^cite_note-718] Jewish masters of the [school of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_of_Paris), such as [Soutine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cha%C3%AFm_Soutine), [Kikoine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Kikoine), [Frenkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitzhak_Frenkel), [Chagall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marc_Chagall) heavily influenced the development of Israeli art.[^cite_note-719][^cite_note-\:43-720] Israeli sculpture took inspiration from modern [European sculpture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_of_Europe) as well [Mesopotamian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_of_Mesopotamia), [Assyrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_sculpture) and local art.[^cite_note-\:6-721][^cite_note-722] [Avraham Melnikov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avraham_Melnikov)'s [The Roaring Lion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Roaring_Lion_\(sculpture\)), David Polus' Alexander Zaid and [Ze'ev Ben Zvi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zeev_Ben-Zvi)'s cubist sculpture exemplify some of the different streams in sculpture.[^cite_note-\:6-721][^cite_note-723][^cite_note-724]&#x20;

Common themes in art are the mystical cities of Safed and Jerusalem, the bohemian café culture of Tel Aviv, agricultural landscapes, biblical stories and war. Today Israeli art has delved into [optical art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optical_art), [AI art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_intelligence_art), [digital art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_art) and the use of salt in sculpture.[^cite_note-\:43-720]&#x20;

### Architecture

Main article: [Architecture of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Israel)



[Bauhaus Museum Tel Aviv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauhaus_Foundation_Tel_Aviv)

Due to the immigration of Jewish architects, architecture has come to reflect different styles. In the early 20th century Jewish architects sought to combine Occidental and Oriental architecture producing buildings that showcase a myriad of infused styles.[^cite_note-725] The [eclectic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eclecticism_in_architecture) style gave way to the modernist [Bauhaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauhaus) style with the influx of German Jewish architects (among them [Erich Mendelsohn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erich_Mendelsohn)) fleeing [Nazi persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_persecution_of_Jews).[^cite_note-726][^cite_note-727] The [White City of Tel Aviv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_City,_Tel_Aviv) is a [UNESCO heritage site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNESCO_Heritage_site).[^cite_note-728] Following independence, multiple government projects were commissioned, a grand part built in a brutalist style with heavy emphasis on the use of concrete and acclimatisation to the desert climate.[^cite_note-729][^cite_note-730] Several novel ideas such as the [Garden City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garden_city_movement) were implemented in Israeli cities; the [Geddes plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geddes_Plan_for_Tel_Aviv) of Tel Aviv became renowned internationally for its revolutionary design and adaptation to the local climate.[^cite_note-731]&#x20;

### Museums

For a more comprehensive list, see [List of Israeli museums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Israeli_museums).



[Shrine of the Book](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrine_of_the_Book), repository of the [Dead Sea Scrolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dead_Sea_Scrolls) in Jerusalem

The [Israel Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Museum) in Jerusalem is one of Israel's most important cultural institutions,[^cite_note-imj-732] and houses the [Dead Sea Scrolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dead_Sea_Scrolls),[^cite_note-733] along with an extensive collection of [Judaica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaica) and [European art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_art).[^cite_note-imj-732] [Yad Vashem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yad_Vashem) ([Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): יָד וַשֵׁם, lit. 'a memorial and a name') is the world's central [Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust) memorial institution and archive of Holocaust-related information.[^cite_note-734] [ANU - Museum of the Jewish People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ANU_-_Museum_of_the_Jewish_People) is an interactive museum devoted to the history of Jewish communities around the world.[^cite_note-735]&#x20;

Israel has the highest number of museums per capita.[^cite_note-736] Several museums are devoted to Islamic culture, including the [Rockefeller Archaeological Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockefeller_Museum) and the [L. A. Mayer Institute for Islamic Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L._A._Mayer_Institute_for_Islamic_Art), both in Jerusalem. The Rockefeller specialises in archaeological remains from Middle East history. It is also the home of the first hominid fossil skull found in West Asia, called [Galilee Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galilee_Man).[^cite_note-737]&#x20;

### Mass media

Main article: [Mass media in Israel](10480502.md)

Notable newspapers include the left-wing *[Haaretz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haaretz)*,[^cite_note-738] centrist *[Yedioth Ahronoth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yedioth_Ahronoth)*,[^cite_note-739] and center-right *[Israel Hayom](12524641.md)*.[^cite_note-740] There are several major television channels which cater to different audiences, from Russian-language [Channel 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Plus)[^cite_note-741] to Arabic-language [Kan 33](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kan_33).[^cite_note-742] The 2024 Freedom House report found Israeli media is "vibrant and free to criticise government policy".[^cite_note-743] In the 2024 [Press Freedom Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Freedom_Index) by [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md), Israel was placed 112th of 180 countries, second in the Middle East and North Africa.[^cite_note-744][^cite_note-RSFNAME-745]&#x20;

Reporters Without Borders reported that the Israel Defence Forces had killed more than 100 Palestinian journalists in Gaza. During the 2023 Gaza war, Israel has been alleged to be "trying to suppress the reporting coming out of the besieged enclave while disinformation infiltrates its own media ecosystem".[^cite_note-RSFNAME-745] In May 2024, Israel shut down the local offices of [Al Jazeera](30204052.md).[^cite_note-746] In 2024, according to the [Committee to Protect Journalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_to_Protect_Journalists), Israel was the second leading country in jailing journalists,[^cite_note-747] and responsible for the majority of journalists killed in the world.[^cite_note-748]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [Israeli cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_cuisine)



A meal including [falafel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falafel), [hummus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hummus), [French fries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_fries) and [Israeli salad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_salad)

Israeli cuisine includes local dishes as well as [Jewish cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_cuisine) brought to the country by immigrants. Particularly since the late 1970s, a [fusion cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusion_cuisine) has developed.[^cite_note-raviv-749] The cuisine has adapted elements of the [Mizrahi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrahi_Jewish_cuisine), [Sephardi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Jewish_cuisine), and [Ashkenazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_cuisine) styles of cooking. It incorporates many foods traditionally eaten in the [Levantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levantine_cuisine), [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_cuisine), [Middle Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Eastern_cuisine) and [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_cuisine) cuisines, such as [falafel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falafel), [hummus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hummus), [shakshouka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shakshouka), [couscous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Couscous), and [za'atar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Za%27atar). [Schnitzel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schnitzel), [pizza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pizza), [hamburgers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamburger), [French fries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_fries), [rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice) and [salad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salad) are common. [Ptitim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_couscous) (Israeli couscous) is a notable Israeli food invented in the 1950s due to rice shortages during the [austerity period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austerity_in_Israel).[^cite_note-JannaGur-750]&#x20;

Roughly half of the Jewish population attests to keeping [kosher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosher) at home.[^cite_note-751][^cite_note-bernstein_227,_233–234-752] [Kosher restaurants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosher_restaurant) make up around a quarter of the total as of 2015.[^cite_note-raviv-749] Pork—often called "white meat" in Israel[^cite_note-bernstein-231-753]—is produced and consumed despite attempts to ban it; [it is forbidden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_restrictions_on_the_consumption_of_pork) by both Judaism and Islam but is permitted by Christianity and mostly produced in traditionally Christian areas of northern Israel.[^cite_note-porkprod-754][^cite_note-755] Other non-kosher foods produced and eaten in Israel include rabbits, ostriches, and non-kosher fish.[^cite_note-bernstein-231-753]&#x20;

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Israel)



[Maccabi Haifa F.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maccabi_Haifa_F.C.) fans at [Sammy Ofer Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sammy_Ofer_Stadium) in the city of Haifa

The most popular spectator sports in Israel are association football and basketball.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETorstrick2004141-756] The [Israeli Premier League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Premier_League) is the country's premier football league, and the [Israeli Basketball Premier League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Basketball_Premier_League) is the premier basketball league.[^cite_note-757] [Maccabi Haifa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maccabi_Haifa_F.C.), [Maccabi Tel Aviv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maccabi_Tel_Aviv_F.C.), [Hapoel Tel Aviv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hapoel_Tel_Aviv_F.C.) and [Beitar Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beitar_Jerusalem_F.C.) are the largest [football clubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_football_clubs_in_Israel). Maccabi Tel Aviv, Maccabi Haifa and Hapoel Tel Aviv have competed in the [UEFA Champions League](44220.md) and Hapoel Tel Aviv reached the [UEFA Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Cup) quarter-finals. Israel hosted and won the [1964 AFC Asian Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_AFC_Asian_Cup); in 1970 the [Israel national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_national_football_team) qualified for the [FIFA World Cup](157227.md), the only time it participated. The [1974 Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_Asian_Games), held in Tehran, were the last Asian Games in which Israel [participated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_at_the_Asian_Games), plagued by Arab countries that [refused to compete](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boycotts_of_Israel_in_sports) with Israel. Israel was excluded from the [1978 Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_Asian_Games) and since then has not competed in Asian sport events.[^cite_note-758] In 1994, [UEFA](32332.md) agreed to admit Israel, and its football teams now compete in Europe. [Maccabi Tel Aviv B.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maccabi_Tel_Aviv_B.C.) has won the [European championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIBA_European_Champions_Cup_and_EuroLeague_records_and_statistics) in basketball six times.[^cite_note-759]&#x20;

Israel has won [20 Olympic medals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_at_the_Olympics) since its first win [in 1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Summer_Olympics), including a gold medal in [windsurfing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sailing_at_the_2004_Summer_Olympics_%E2%80%93_Men%27s_Mistral_One_Design) at the [2004 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Summer_Olympics),[^cite_note-760] and seven medals at the 2024 Paris Olympics.[^cite_note-761] Israel has won [over 100](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_at_the_Paralympics) gold medals in the [Paralympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paralympic_Games) and is ranked 20th in the [all-time medal count](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-time_Paralympic_Games_medal_table). The [1968 Summer Paralympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Summer_Paralympics) were hosted by Israel.[^cite_note-762] The [Maccabiah Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maccabiah_Games), an Olympic-style event for [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Jews_in_sports) and Israeli athletes, has been held every four years since the 1930s. [Krav Maga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krav_Maga), a martial art developed by Jewish ghetto defenders, is used by the Israeli security forces and police.[^cite_note-763]&#x20;

[Chess](5134.md) is a leading sport. There are many Israeli grandmasters and [Israeli chess players](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Israeli_chess_players) have won a number of youth world championships.[^cite_note-764] Israel stages an annual international [championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Chess_Championship) and hosted the [World Team Chess Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Team_Chess_Championship) in 2005.&#x20;

[^cite_note-38]: &#x20;Meir-Glitzenstein, Esther (Fall 2018). "Turning Points in the Historiography of Jewish Immigration from Arab Countries to Israel". *Israel Studies*. **23** (3). Indiana University Press: 114–122. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.2979/israelstudies.23.3.15](https://doi.org/10.2979%2Fisraelstudies.23.3.15). [JSTOR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JSTOR_\(identifier\)) [10.2979/israelstudies.23.3.15](https://www.jstor.org/stable/10.2979/israelstudies.23.3.15). [S2CID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S2CID_\(identifier\)) [150208821](https://api.semanticscholar.org/CorpusID\:150208821). The mass immigration from Arab countries began in mid-1949 and included three communities that relocated to Israel almost in their entirety: 31,000 Jews from Libya, 50,000 from Yemen, and 125,000 from Iraq. Additional immigrants arrived from Egypt, Morocco, Tunisia, Turkey, Iran, India, and elsewhere. Within three years, the Jewish population of Israel doubled. The ethnic composition of the population shifted as well, as immigrants from Muslim counties and their offspring now comprised one third of the Jewish population—an unprecedented phenomenon in global immigration history. From 1952–60, Israel regulated and restricted immigration from Muslim countries with a selective immigration policy based on economic criteria, and sent these immigrants, most of whom were North African, to peripheral Israeli settlements. The selective immigration policy ended in 1961 when, following an agreement between Israel and Morocco, about 100,000 Jews immigrated to the State. From 1952–68 about 600,000 Jews arrived in Israel, three quarters of whom were from Arab countries and the remaining immigrants were largely from Eastern Europe. Today fewer than 30,000 remain in Muslim countries, mostly concentrated in Iran and Turkey.

Retrieved 22 December 2025. Para. 233: the Commission concludes that the 'direct and public incitement to commit genocide' under article III(c) of the Genocide Convention is established. Para. 252. The Commission concludes on reasonable grounds that the Israeli authorities and Israeli security forces have committed and are continuing to commit the following actus reus of genocide against the Palestinians in the Gaza Strip, namely (i) killing members of the group; (ii) causing serious bodily or mental harm to members of the group; (iii) deliberately inflicting on the group conditions of life calculated to bring about its physical destruction in whole or in part; and (iv) imposing measures intended to prevent births within the group [""More than a human can bear": Israel's systematic use of sexual, reproductive and other forms of gender-based violence since 7 October 2023"](https://www.ohchr.org/sites/default/files/documents/hrbodies/hrcouncil/sessions-regular/session58/a-hrc-58-crp-6.pdf) (PDF). *UN Human Rights Council*. 13 March 2025. Retrieved 22 December 2025. Para. 175: The Commission concludes that the destruction of the Basma IVF clinic was a measure intended to prevent births among Palestinians in Gaza, which is a genocidal act under the Rome Statute and Genocide Convention. Para. 178: The Commission concludes that the ISF caused serious bodily and mental harm to members of this group, and deliberately inflicted conditions of life that were calculated to bring about the physical destruction of Palestinians in Gaza as a group, in whole or in part, which are categories of genocidal acts in the Rome Statute and the Genocide Convention. Para. 218: The Commission finds that the Israeli authorities have destroyed in part the reproductive capacity of the Palestinians in Gaza as a group, including by imposing measures intended to prevent births, one of the categories of genocidal acts in the Rome Statute and the Genocide Convention. Para. 219: The harm for pregnant, lactating and new mothers is of an unprecedented scale in Gaza...The underlying acts amount to crimes against humanity and deliberately inflicting conditions of life calculated to bring about the physical destruction of Palestinians as a group, one of the categories of genocidal acts in the Rome Statute and the Genocide Convention ["UN Independent International Commission of Inquiry, legal analysis and recommendations on the implementation of the International Court of Justice Advisory Opinion, Legal Consequences arising from the Policies and Practices of Israel in the Occupied Palestinian Territory"](https://www.ohchr.org/sites/default/files/documents/hrbodies/hrcouncil/coiopt/2024-10-18-COI-position-paper_co-israel.pdf) (PDF). *UN Human Rights Council*. 18 October 2024. Retrieved 22 December 2025. Para. 23: On the issue of genocide, the Commission notes the provisional measures orders issued by the Court in the Application of the Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide in the Gaza Strip (South Africa v. Israel). Thus, the Commission finds that, unless States cease their aid and assistance to Israel in the commission of these acts, those States shall be deemed to be complicit in those internationally wrongful acts. As a West Semitic personal name it existed long before it became a tribal or a geographical name. This is not without significance, though is it rarely mentioned. We learn of a maryanu named ysr"il (\*Yi¡sr—a"ilu) from Ugarit living in the same period, but the name was already used a thousand years before in Ebla. The word Israel originated as a West Semitic personal name. One of the many names that developed into the name of the ancestor of a clan, of a tribe and finally of a people and a nation.

In the south, Adora and Marisa were conquered; (Aristobulus') primary accomplishment was annexing and Judaizing the region of Iturea, located between the Lebanon and Anti-Lebanon mountains

[^cite_note-106]: &#x20;Ben-Sasson, H.H. (1976). [*A History of the Jewish People*](https://books.google.com/books?id=2kSovzudhFUC\&pg=PA226). Harvard University Press. p. 226. [^cite_note-110]: &#x20;Raviv, Dvir; Ben David, Chaim (2021). ["Cassius Dio's figures for the demographic consequences of the Bar Kokhba War: Exaggeration or reliable account?"](https://doi.org/10.1017%2FS1047759421000271). *Journal of Roman Archaeology*. **34** (2): 585–607. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1017/S1047759421000271](https://doi.org/10.1017%2FS1047759421000271). [ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_\(identifier\)) [1047-7594](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/1047-7594). [S2CID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S2CID_\(identifier\)) [245512193](https://api.semanticscholar.org/CorpusID\:245512193). Scholars have long doubted the historical accuracy of Cassius Dio's account of the consequences of the Bar Kokhba War (Roman History 69.14). According to this text, considered the most reliable literary source for the Second Jewish Revolt, the war encompassed all of Judea: the Romans destroyed 985 villages and 50 fortresses, and killed 580,000 rebels. This article reassesses Cassius Dio's figures by drawing on new evidence from excavations and surveys in Judea, Transjordan, and the Galilee. Three research methods are combined: an ethno-archaeological comparison with the settlement picture in the Ottoman Period, comparison with similar settlement studies in the Galilee, and an evaluation of settled sites from the Middle Roman Period (70–136 CE). The study demonstrates the potential contribution of the archaeological record to this issue and supports the view of Cassius Dio's demographic data as a reliable account, which he based on contemporaneous documentation.

[^cite_note-:02-111]: &#x20;Mor, Menahem (18 April 2016). *The Second Jewish Revolt*. BRILL. pp. 483–484. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1163/9789004314634](https://doi.org/10.1163%2F9789004314634). [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-90-04-31463-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-90-04-31463-4). Land confiscation in Judaea was part of the suppression of the revolt policy of the Romans and punishment for the rebels. But the very claim that the sikarikon laws were annulled for settlement purposes seems to indicate that Jews continued to reside in Judaea even after the Second Revolt. There is no doubt that this area suffered the severest damage from the suppression of the revolt. Settlements in Judaea, such as Herodion and Bethar, had already been destroyed during the course of the revolt, and Jews were expelled from the districts of Gophna, Herodion, and Aqraba. However, it should not be claimed that the region of Judaea was completely destroyed. Jews continued to live in areas such as Lod (Lydda), south of the Hebron Mountain, and the coastal regions. In other areas of the Land of Israel that did not have any direct connection with the Second Revolt, no settlement changes can be identified as resulting from it.

[^cite_note-:1-120]: &#x20;Ehrlich, Michael (2022). *The Islamization of the Holy Land, 634–1800*. Arc Humanities Press. pp. 3–4. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-64189-222-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-64189-222-3). [OCLC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OCLC_\(identifier\)) [1302180905](https://search.worldcat.org/oclc/1302180905). The Jewish community strove to recover from the catastrophic results of the Bar Kokhva revolt (132–135 CE). Although some of these attempts were relatively successful, the Jews never fully recovered. During the Late Roman and Byzantine periods, many Jews emigrated to thriving centres in the diaspora, especially Iraq, whereas some converted to Christianity and others continued to live in the Holy Land, especially in Galilee and the coastal plain. During the Byzantine period, the three provinces of Palestine included more than thirty cities, namely, settlements with a bishop see. After the Muslim conquest in the 630s, most of these cities declined and eventually disappeared. As a result, in many cases the local ecclesiastical administration weakened, while in others it simply ceased to exist. Consequently, many local Christians converted to Islam. Thus, almost twelve centuries later, when the army led by Napoleon Bonaparte arrived in the Holy Land, most of the local population was Muslim.

The destruction of the Jewish metropolis of Jerusalem and its environs and the eventual refounding of the city... had lasting repercussions. \[...] However, in other parts of Palestine the Jewish population remained strong \[...] What does seem clear is a different kind of change. Immigration of Christians and the conversion of pagans, Samaritans and Jews eventually produced a Christian majority

[^cite_note-122]: &#x20;Bar, Doron (2003). "The Christianisation of Rural Palestine during Late Antiquity". *The Journal of Ecclesiastical History*. **54** (3): 401–421. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1017/s0022046903007309](https://doi.org/10.1017%2Fs0022046903007309). The dominant view of the history of Palestine during the Byzantine period links the early phases of the consecration of the land during the fourth century and the substantial external financial investment that accompanied the building of churches on holy sites on the one hand with the Christianisation of the population on the other. Churches were erected primarily at the holy sites, 12 while at the same time Palestine's position and unique status as the Christian 'Holy Land' became more firmly rooted. All this, coupled with immigration and conversion, allegedly meant that the Christianisation of Palestine took place much more rapidly than that of other areas of the Roman empire, brought in its wake the annihilation of the pagan cults and meant that by the middle of the fifth century there was a clear Christian majority.

Another major cause of antagonism was the labor controversy. The hard core of Second Aliyah socialists, who were to become the Yishuv's leaders in the 1920s and 1930s, believed that the settler economy must not depend on or exploit Arab labor... But, in reality, rather than "meshing," the nationalist ethos had simply overpowered and driven out the socialist ethos... There were other reasons for the "conquest of labor." The socialists of the Second Aliyah used the term to denote three things: overcoming the Jews' traditional remove from agricultural labor and helping them transform into the "new Jews"; struggling against employers for better conditions; and replacing Arabs with Jews in manual jobs.

In addition, according to British records relied upon by the ad hoc committee, the Jewish population possessed registered ownership of only 5.6 per cent of Palestine, and was eclipsed by the Arabs in land ownership in every one of Palestine's 16 sub-districts. Moreover, the quality of the land granted to the proposed Jewish state was highly skewed in its favour. UNSCOP reported that under its majority plan "\[t]he Jews will have the more economically developed part of the country embracing practically the whole of the citrus-producing area"—Palestine's staple export crop—even though approximately half of the citrus-bearing land was owned by the Arabs. In addition, according to updated British records submitted to the ad hoc committee's two sub-committees, "of the irrigated, cultivable areas" of the country, 84 per cent would be in the Jewish State and 16 per cent would be in the Arab State"."[Morris 2008](#CITEREFMorris2008), p. 75: "The night of 29–30 November passed in the Yishuv's settlements in noisy public rejoicing. Most had sat glued to their radio sets broadcasting live from Flushing Meadow. A collective cry of joy went up when the two-thirds mark was achieved: a state had been sanctioned by the international community."

Although they were of the view that the Jewish national home promised in the Mandate was equivalent to a Jewish state, they well understood that such a claim could not be maintained under prevailing international law. \[...] Based on its own terms, it is impossible to escape the conclusion that the partition plan privileged European interests over those of Palestine's indigenous people and, as such, was an embodiment of the Eurocentricity of the international system that was allegedly a thing of the past. For this reason, the Arabs took a more principled position in line with prevailing international law, rejecting partition outright. This rejection has disingenuously been presented in some of the literature as indicative of political intransigence,<sup>69</sup> and even hostility towards the Jews as Jews."

Retrieved 1 December 2018. the removal of the Egyptian blockade of the Straits of Tiran at the entrance of the Gulf of Aqaba. The blockade closed Israel's sea lane to East Africa and the Far East, hindering the development of Israel's southern port of Eilat and its hinterland, the Nege. Another important objective of the Israeli war plan was the elimination of the terrorist bases in the Gaza Strip, from which daily fedayeen incursions into Israel made life unbearable for its southern population. And last but not least, the concentration of the Egyptian forces in the Sinai Peninsula, armed with the newly acquired weapons from the Soviet bloc, prepared for an attack on Israel. Here, Ben-Gurion believed, was a time bomb that had to be defused before it was too late. Reaching the Suez Canal did not figure at all in Israel's war objectives.

[^cite_note-233]: &#x20;Dominic Joseph Caraccilo (2011). [*Beyond Guns and Steel: A War Termination Strategy*](https://books.google.com/books?id=FDA8dQyaQ9MC\&pg=PAPA113). ABC-CLIO. pp. 113–. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-313-39149-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-313-39149-1). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20231219071150/https://books.google.com/books?id=FDA8dQyaQ9MC\&pg=PAPA113) from the original on 19 December 2023. Retrieved 1 December 2018. The escalation continued with the Egyptian blockade of the Straits of Tiran, and Nasser's nationalization of the Suez Canal in July 1956. On October 14, Nasser made clear his intent:"I am not solely fighting against Israel itself. My task is to deliver the Arab world from destruction through Israel's intrigue, which has its roots abroad. Our hatred is very strong. There is no sense in talking about peace with Israel. There is not even the smallest place for negotiations." Less than two weeks later, on October 25, Egypt signed a tripartite agreement with Syria and Jordan placing Nasser in command of all three armies. The continued blockade of the Suez Canal and Gulf of Aqaba to Israeli shipping, combined with the increased fedayeen attacks and the bellicosity of recent Arab statements, prompted Israel, with the backing of Britain and France, to attack Egypt on October 29, 1956.

Retrieved 28 October 2024.[Albanese, Francesca](72566699.md) (25 March 2024). [Anatomy of a Genocide: Report of the Special Rapporteur on the situation of human rights in the Palestinian territories occupied since 1967, Francesca Albanese](https://documents.un.org/doc/undoc/gen/g24/046/11/pdf/g2404611.pdf) (PDF) (Report). [United Nations Special Rapporteur on the occupied Palestinian territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Special_Rapporteur_on_the_occupied_Palestinian_territories). p. 1. By analysing the patterns of violence and Israeli policies in its onslaught on Gaza, the present report concludes that there are reasonable grounds to believe that the threshold indicating that Israel has committed genocide has been met[Amnesty International](18947898.md) (2024). ['You Feel Like You Are Subhuman': Israel's Genocide Against Palestinians In Gaza](https://amnesty.ca/wp-content/uploads/2024/12/Amnesty-International-Gaza-Genocide-Report-December-4-2024.pdf) (PDF) (Report). p. 13. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20241205121850/https://amnesty.ca/wp-content/uploads/2024/12/Amnesty-International-Gaza-Genocide-Report-December-4-2024.pdf) (PDF) from the original on 5 December 2024. This report focuses on the Israeli authorities' policies and actions in Gaza as part of the military offensive they launched in the wake of the Hamas-led attacks on 7 October 2023 while situating them within the broader context of Israel's unlawful occupation, and system of apartheid against Palestinians in Gaza, the West Bank, including East Jerusalem, and Israel. It assesses allegations of violations and crimes under international law by Israel in Gaza within the framework of genocide under international law, concluding that there is sufficient evidence to believe that Israel's conduct in Gaza following 7 October 2023 amounts to genocide.[Traverso, Enzo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enzo_Traverso) (2024). [*Gaza Faces History*](https://books.google.com/books?id=7-8PEQAAQBAJ). Other Press. p. 8. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-63542-555-0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-63542-555-0). The only normative definition we have, codified at the United Nations Genocide Convention of 1948, accurately describes the current situation in Palestine ... describes exactly what is happening in Gaza today["One year of denouncing the genocide of Palestinians in Gaza"](https://www.fidh.org/en/region/north-africa-middle-east/israel-palestine/one-year-of-denouncing-the-genocide-of-palestinians-in-gaza). *[International Federation for Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Federation_for_Human_Rights)*. 12 December 2024. Retrieved 4 June 2025. One year ago, the FIDH International Board, its governing body elected by all its member organisations, recognised, after extensive debate and examination, that Israel was carrying out genocide against the Palestinian people in Gaza[B'Tselem 2025](#CITEREFB'Tselem2025), p. 86: "The review presented in this report leaves no room for doubt: since October 2023, the Israeli regime has been responsible for carrying out genocide against the Palestinians in the Gaza Strip. Killing tens of thousands of people; causing bodily or mental harm to hundreds of thousands more; destroying homes and civilian infrastructure on a massive scale; starvation, displacement, and denying humanitarian aid — all this is being perpetrated systematically, as part of a coordinated attack aimed at annihilating all facets of life in the Gaza Strip."

Archived from [the original](https://www.ohchr.org/en/press-releases/2023/11/gaza-un-experts-call-international-community-prevent-genocide-against) on 24 December 2023. Retrieved 22 December 2023. Grave violations committed by Israel against Palestinians in the aftermath of 7 October, particularly in Gaza, point to a genocide in the making, UN experts said today. They illustrated evidence of increasing genocidal incitement, overt intent to "destroy the Palestinian people under occupation", loud calls for a 'second Nakba' in Gaza and the rest of the occupied Palestinian territory, and the use of powerful weaponry with inherently indiscriminate impacts, resulting in a colossal death toll and destruction of life-sustaining infrastructure.

[^cite_note-368]: &#x20;Charbit, Denis (2014). ["Israel's Self-Restrained Secularism from the 1947 Status Quo Letter to the Present"](https://books.google.com/books?id=gThvBAAAQBAJ\&pg=PAPA167). In Berlinerblau, Jacques; Fainberg, Sarah; Nou, Aurora (eds.). *Secularism on the Edge: Rethinking Church-State Relations in the United States, France, and Israel*. Palgrave Macmillan. pp. 167–169. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-137-38115-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-137-38115-6). The compromise, therefore, was to choose constructive ambiguity: as surprising as it may seem, there is no law that declares Judaism the official religion of Israel. However, there is no other law that declares Israel's neutrality toward all confessions. Judaism is not recognized as the official religion of the state, and even though the Jewish, Muslim and Christian clergy receive their salaries from the state, this fact does not make Israel a neutral state. This apparent pluralism cannot dissimulate the fact that Israel displays a clear and undoubtedly hierarchical pluralism in religious matters. ... It is important to note that from a multicultural point of view, this self-restrained secularism allows Muslim law to be practiced in Israel for personal matters of the Muslim community. As surprising as it seems, if not paradoxical for a state in war, Israel is the only Western democratic country in which Sharia enjoys such an official status.

[^cite_note-369]: &#x20;Sharot, Stephen (2007). ["Judaism in Israel: Public Religion, Neo-Traditionalism, Messianism, and Ethno-Religious Conflict"](https://books.google.com/books?id=vA8edg7bv0kC\&pg=PAPA671). In Beckford, James A.; Demerath, Jay (eds.). *The Sage Handbook of the Sociology of Religion*. Sage Publications. pp. 671–672. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-4129-1195-5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-4129-1195-5). It is true that Jewish Israelis, and secular Israelis in particular, conceive of religion as shaped by a state-sponsored religious establishment. There is no formal state religion in Israel, but the state gives its official recognition and financial support to particular religious communities, Jewish, Islamic and Christian, whose religious authorities and courts are empowered to deal with matters of personal status and family law, such as marriage, divorce, and alimony, that are binding on all members of the communities.

[^cite_note-370]: &#x20;Jacoby, Tami Amanda (2005). [*Women in Zones of Conflict: Power and Resistance in Israel*](https://books.google.com/books?id=pr1LJNrlmuIC\&pg=PAPA53). McGill-Queen's University Press. pp. 53–54. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-7735-2993-9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-7735-2993-9). Although there is no official religion in Israel, there is also no clear separation between religion and state. In Israeli public life, tensions frequently arise among different streams of Judaism: Ultra-Orthodox, National-Religious, *Mesorati* (Conservative), Reconstructionist Progressive (Reform), and varying combinations of traditionalism and non-observance. Despite this variety in religious observances in society, Orthodox Judaism prevails institutionally over the other streams. This boundary is an historical consequence of the unique evolution of the relationship between Israel nationalism and state building. ... Since the founding period, in order to defuse religious tensions, the State of Israel has adopted what is known as the 'status quo,' an unwritten agreement stipulating that no further changes would be made in the status of religion, and that conflict between the observant and non-observant sectors would be handled circumstantially. The 'status quo' has since pertained to the legal status of both religious and secular Jews in Israel. This situation was designed to appease the religious sector, and has been upheld indefinitely through the disproportionate power of religious political parties in all subsequent coalition governments. ... On one hand, the Declaration of Independence adopted in 1948 explicitly guarantees freedom of religion. On the other, it simultaneously prevents the separation of religion and state in Israel.

["Israel: The security barrier—between international law, constitutional law, and domestic judicial review"](https://doi.org/10.1093%2Ficon%2Fmol021). *International Journal of Constitutional Law*. **4** (3): 548. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1093/icon/mol021](https://doi.org/10.1093%2Ficon%2Fmol021). The real controversy hovering over all the litigation on the security barrier concerns the fate of the Israeli settlements in the occupied territories. Since 1967, Israel has allowed and even encouraged its citizens to live in the new settlements established in the territories, motivated by religious and national sentiments attached to the history of the Jewish nation in the land of Israel. This policy has also been justified in terms of security interests, taking into consideration the dangerous geographic circumstances of Israel before 1967 (where Israeli areas on the Mediterranean coast were potentially threatened by Jordanian control of the West Bank ridge). The international community, for its part, has viewed this policy as patently illegal, based on the provisions of the Fourth Geneva Convention that prohibit moving populations to or from territories under occupation.

Retrieved 22 November 2015. And even when no Israelis are involved, few countries are as fast as Israel in mobilizing entire delegations to rush to the other side of the world. It has been proved time and again in recent years, after the earthquake in Haiti, the typhoon in the Philippines and the quake/tsunami/nuclear disaster in Japan. For a country of Israel's size and resources, without conveniently located aircraft carriers and overseas bases, it is quite an impressive achievement.

(p.28) "The positions of the participants in the focus groups reflect the strength of Palestinian-Arab identity among Arab citizens and the fact that they do not see a contradiction between Palestinian-Arab national identity and Israeli civic identity. The collective position presented in the focus group discussions finds expression in the public sphere and emphasizes the Palestinian national identity. Conversely, the responses of the survey participants reveal individual attitudes that assign a broader (albeit secondary, identity) dimension to the component of Israeli civic identity"; quote (p.25): The designation "Arab citizens of Israel" was acceptable to them on the basis of the understanding that it is impossible to live without citizenship, and as long as Israeli citizenship does not harm the national consciousness.
